<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p">
<div class="fn" id="TF5"><p>Note that none of these studies was done in mice.</p></div>
<div class="fn" id="TF6"><p>C1, 1 CS (tone/light); C2, 2 CSs (reinforced tone); T, 1 CS tone; T-T, tone-tone; T-L, tone-light; F + I, frequency + intensity; A, ascending tone; D, descending tone; S, steady tone; FL, forelimb; MCP, middle cerebellar peduncle; MF, mossy fibers; PCX, picrotoxin; TSE, total stimulus energy; LGN,lateral geniculate nucleus; HP, hippocampus; CTX, cortex</p></div>
</div></section></section><section id="s5C"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">CR amplitude</h3>
<p>Similar to CR percentage, the amplitude of conditioned eyelid closure calculated across all CS trials shows a stepwise decrease when the difference between the trained CS and GS increases. Since CR probability and amplitude of eyelid closure show a strong covariation on a single session level, this is not a surprising result. Indeed, previous studies done in rabbits, show the same phenomenon for eyelid closure calculated over all trials. For instance, <a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">Garcia et al. (2003)</a> and <a href="#B67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67">Ohyama et al. (2003)</a> report that the magnitude (i.e., CR amplitude calculated over all CS trials) of eyeblink responses shows a progressive decrease with each incremental step in tone frequency away from the CS. In addition, <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a>, using electrical stimulation of mossy fibers as CS, similarly show that GSs with stimulation frequencies lower than the CS result in a lower CR amplitude.</p>
<p>Only a subset of previous studies also describes the amplitude of eyelid closure for only those trials wherein the animal shows a CR (<a href="#B41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41">Kehoe et al., 1995</a>; <a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">Garcia et al., 2003</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al., 2018</a>). Looking at this value in our study, using a 0.05 CR criterion, we again observed the same gradient with the highest CR amplitude to the trained CS and a progressive decrease in CR amplitude for each incremental or decremental 2-kHz step away from the CS. Although this gradient was less steep than for CR amplitude calculated over all trials, we could not establish the clear binary choice phenomenon reported by <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a>, whereby the probability of CRs gradually decreased on the degree of similarity between the GS and CS, but the amplitude of the CR remained constant. Since a FEC of 0.05 is an arbitrary CR threshold, we also looked at higher CR thresholds (<a href="#F8" class="usa-link">Fig. 8<em>B–E</em></a>) but could not establish the binary choice. The most parsimonious explanation for this discrepancy between our study and <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a> is the difference in the eyelid motor plant between mice and rabbits. In mice, the main force driving eyeblink CRs comes from contraction of the orbicularis oculi muscle, while in rabbits (and humans) there is, in addition to the contraction of the orbicularis oculi muscle, a more pronounced role for a simultaneous relaxation of the levator palpebrae muscle (<a href="#B93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B93">Ansari and Nadeem, 2016</a>). This results in a different CR expression profiles, whereby conditioned rabbits show a clear bimodal (or better: zero-inflated) distribution of CR amplitudes (<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">Garcia et al., 2003</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al., 2018</a>). For mice, this bimodal distribution is present, but clearly less obvious compared with the ones reported for rabbits (<a href="#B44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44">Kloth et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#B83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83">ten Brinke et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#B4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4">Albergaria et al., 2018</a>; compare histograms in <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4<em>D</em>,<em>F</em></a> with those reported by <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al., 2018</a>, their Fig. 2<em>A</em>).</p>
<p>Another difference between our study and the <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a> study is the performance level of the animals at the end of training. <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a> trained their rabbits “until both CR percentage was high (CR% &gt; 90%) and CR amplitudes were robust and near the target amplitude.” In practice, this meant that most rabbits were trained for 10 sessions. In our experiment, mice reached maximum conditioning levels of ∼70% CRs and CR amplitudes of ∼0.5. These values for our mice did not further increase and remained stable after acquisition session 8. Thus, the <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a> rabbits were clearly performing better than our mice: the rabbits were overtrained, whereas in our mice there was theoretically still room for further improvement. This difference in performance level could also explain why <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al. (2018)</a> report a binary choice and we observe more a continuum of responses.</p></section><section id="s5D"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">CR timing</h3>
<p>Most previous studies on stimulus generalization during eyeblink conditioning ignored the adaptive timing properties of eyelid CRs (<a href="#B60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60">Moore, 1964</a>; <a href="#B76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76">Siegel et al., 1968</a>; <a href="#B50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50">Liu, 1971</a>; <a href="#B61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61">Moore and Mis, 1973</a>; <a href="#B42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42">Khilkevich et al., 2018</a>). Our data show that mice CRs peaked significantly later to GSs with higher frequencies compared with those with lower frequencies. These findings are in line with response patterns described by <a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">Garcia et al. (2003)</a>. Interestingly, when electrical stimulation of the forelimb, that had served as a CS, was suddenly switched from 50 kHz to a 100-kHz stimulus train, an opposite effect was reported: the latency to CR peak was shorter for the higher frequency stimulus (<a href="#B81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B81">Svensson et al., 1997</a>). We have no clear explanation for this effect of tone frequency on latency to CR peak. It may reflect processing of auditory information between the level of sensory organs (cochlea) and the cerebellar mossy fiber input system.</p>
<p>In mice, there is no effect of tone frequency on the latency to CR onset. This finding is in line with previous work in mice, showing that the latency to CR onset is rather unaffected by the duration of the CS (<a href="#B13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13">Chettih et al., 2011</a>), which is another difference between eyeblink CRs in mice compared with other species (rabbits, humans, ferrets). Indeed, a trending (but not significant) increase in CR onset latency has been described for stimulus generalization in rabbits (<a href="#B27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27">Garcia et al., 2003</a>).</p></section><section id="s5E"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Latent inhibition</h3>
<p>Three out of fourteen animals did not learn the task within the 10 d of acquisition training, which is slightly higher compared with previous work by our group (<a href="#B11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11">Boele et al., 2018</a>; <a href="#B29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29">Grasselli et al., 2020</a>; <a href="#B7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7">Beekhof et al., 2021</a>; <a href="#B14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14">de Oude et al., 2021</a>). The difference between this study and previous work, is the amount of CS preexposure during the 10 baseline sessions, which potentially leads to “latent inhibition.” Latent inhibition is the phenomenon whereby it takes longer to get conditioned to a familiar stimulus than to novel stimulus. The preexposure to the CS (20 in total) and GS (20 in total for each frequency) during the 10 baseline sessions could explain why these three animals did not learn the task. Although we made sure all animals received the exact same amount of CS-only and GS-only trials during the baseline sessions to keep the novelty level of the tone equal for all animals, it could be that the latent inhibition effect varies between animals.</p></section><section id="s5F"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Comparison between generalization curves from eyeblink conditioning and fear conditioning</h3>
<p>Stimulus generalization has been studied previously using fear conditioning. However, to our knowledge, a complete assessment of conditioned fear responses as a function of a wide range of tone frequency is missing: all studies probed generalization of fear responses presenting only one or a few novel auditory cue(s) (<a href="#B75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75">Shaban et al., 2006</a>; <a href="#B91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B91">Zhang et al., 2019</a>). In addition, most fear conditioning studies use a differential paradigm during acquisition. For these two reasons combined, it is almost impossible to compare our eyeblink conditioning generalization curve with those using fear conditioning. It would be interesting to find out how US intensity affects the shape of the generalization curve. One could do so using eyeblink conditioning, using a stronger US, which is known to induce more fear (<a href="#B8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8">Boele et al., 2010</a>) and leads to faster acquisition (<a href="#B68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68">Passey, 1948</a>; <a href="#B80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B80">Spence et al., 1953</a>; <a href="#B78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78">Smith, 1968</a>; <a href="#B25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25">Freeman et al., 1993</a>; <a href="#B40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40">Kehoe and White, 2002</a>; see <a href="#B10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10">Boele et al., 2016</a>). Based on work done on fear conditioning (<a href="#B48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48">Laxmi et al., 2003</a>; <a href="#B20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20">Dunsmoor et al., 2009</a>, <a href="#B21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21">2017</a>), we predict that a more aversive US leads to stronger generalization (i.e., a less steep gradient). In addition, the training paradigm (differential vs nondifferential training) has effects on the shape of the curve (<a href="#B19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19">Dunsmoor and LaBar, 2013</a>).</p></section><section id="s5G"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Neural mechanisms</h3>
<p>The study of stimulus generalization primarily comes from fields of ethology or experimental psychology and has been investigated with various experimental paradigms other than Pavlovian eyeblink conditioning, such as fear conditioning and operant conditioning. These investigations on stimulus generalization have been performed in many species including humans, goldfishes, rats and pigeons, and generally report a decreasing generalization gradient when moving away from the trained stimulus (<a href="#B84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B84">Thomas and Mitchell, 1962</a>; <a href="#B6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6">Baron, 1973</a>; <a href="#B28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28">Ghirlanda and Enquist, 2003</a>), similar to what we found in Pavlovian eyeblink conditioning in mice. Interestingly, <a href="#B100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B100">Guttman and Kalish (1956)</a> showed that stimulus generalization does not originate from a failure in perceptual discrimination, but instead it is an active process. This principle probably also applies to cerebellar learning rules during eyeblink conditioning. Although early reports on stimulus generalization in eyeblink conditioning have shown that lesions of noncerebellar structures, for instance hippocampus or cerebral cortex, affect eyeblink conditioning and stimulus generalization in eyeblink conditioning (<a href="#B79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79">Solomon and Moore, 1975</a>), the leading idea now is that the essential eyeblink conditioning memory trace is formed in cerebellum (<a href="#B56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56">McCormick et al., 1981</a>, <a href="#B57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57">1982</a>; <a href="#B54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54">Mauk and Donegan, 1997</a>; <a href="#B90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B90">Yeo and Hesslow, 1998</a>; <a href="#B55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55">Mauk and Buonomano, 2004</a>; <a href="#B24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24">Freeman and Steinmetz, 2011</a>; <a href="#B33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33">Heiney et al., 2014</a>; <a href="#B23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23">Freeman, 2015</a>; <a href="#B82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82">ten Brinke et al., 2015</a>). Purkinje cells in well-defined microzones in cerebellar cortex receive converging inputs from the mossy fiber – parallel fiber pathway, which transmits the CS, and the climbing fiber pathway, which transmits the US (<a href="#B15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15">De Zeeuw and Ten Brinke, 2015</a>; <a href="#B16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16">De Zeeuw et al., 2021</a>). Repeated pairing of CS and US leads to the acquisition of a simple spike pause in Purkinje cells in response to the CS (<a href="#B66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66">Ohmae and Medina, 2015</a>; <a href="#B82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B82">ten Brinke et al., 2015</a>; <a href="#B39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39">Jirenhed et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#B64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64">Narain et al., 2018</a>). Although further research is needed, one could imagine that the higher and lower frequency tones are not equally represented in the parallel fiber beams and thereby contributing to the asymmetric distribution in the stimulus-response relation.</p>
<p>The simple spike pause in turn causes a temporary disinhibition of cerebellar nuclei neurons, which (indirectly) innervate the motor neurons that control the eyelid musculature (<a href="#B30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30">Halverson et al., 2015</a>, <a href="#B31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31">2018</a>; <a href="#B39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39">Jirenhed et al., 2017</a>; <a href="#B83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B83">ten Brinke et al., 2017</a>). In addition, mossy fiber and climbing fibers send of collaterals directly to the cerebellar nuclei. Our previous work has shown that the number of varicosities on these mossy fiber collaterals in the cerebellar nuclei increases quite robustly with eyeblink conditioning (<a href="#B9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9">Boele et al., 2013</a>). Moreover, the number of these varicosities correlates positively with the amplitude of eyelid CRs, indicating that these mossy fibers are important for CR expression. Work by Ohyama and colleagues has shown that pharmaceutical disconnection of Purkinje cell inhibition from the cerebellar nuclei results in much smaller CRs to GSs, but that CRs to the trained CS remained largely the same in size, although the adaptive timing of these CRs was affected (<a href="#B67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67">Ohyama et al., 2003</a>). This suggests that mossy fiber collaterals form a CS-specific pathway from the pontine nuclei to the cerebellar nuclei. We hypothesize that cerebellar cortex and nuclei play synergistic roles in CR expression and timing. GSs that resemble the CS will result in a rather similar and strong neural representation in the parallel fiber input at the Purkinje cell, resulting in a rather similar simple spike pause. GSs that are more different instead, will result in a weaker representation, leading to a weaker Purkinje cell response.</p></section></section><section id="s6"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Synthesis</h2>
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="boxed-text1"><p>Reviewing Editor: Michaël Zugaro, CNRS, Collège de France, Inserm</p>
<p>Decisions are customarily a result of the Reviewing Editor and the peer reviewers coming together and discussing their recommendations until a consensus is reached. When revisions are invited, a fact-based synthesis statement explaining their decision and outlining what is needed to prepare a revision will be listed below. The following reviewer(s) agreed to reveal their identity: John Freeman, Zsuzsanna Callaerts-Vegh. Note: If this manuscript was transferred from JNeurosci and a decision was made to accept the manuscript without peer review, a brief statement to this effect will instead be what is listed below.</p></section><p>The manuscript has been evaluated by two reviewers. Here is a summary of their comments.</p>
<p>Stimulus generalization has been extensively studied in the context of fear conditioning as well as of clinical issues and solutions (i.e. phobias or extinction learning). Here, the authors present stimulus generalization in a different context, namely to auditory stimuli in a cerebellar learning paradigm (conditioned eyeblink).</p>
<p>The experiment used very precise and rigorous methods to condition mice with a 10 kHz tone followed by generalization sessions with non-reinforced presentations of lower- and higher-frequency tones (care was taken to avoid eliciting beta responses, which interfere with CR measurement in mice). The study is well executed and involves a series of important control experiments and validations, including the percentage of CRs, CR magnitude, CR amplitude, latency to peak, and onset latency. While generalization is seen across auditory frequencies, CR percentage and amplitude decrease as the test stimulus frequency differs from the trained frequency. The findings are generally consistent with the findings from rabbits, ferrets, and humans, and are very interesting from a mechanistic point of view.</p>
<p>The reviews are supportive, but raise a number of issues which should be fairly straightforward to address.</p>
<p>Major issues</p>
<p>The paper does not explicitly indicate why the findings are important in either the abstract or discussion. For example, fear conditioning plays an important role in many clinical pathologies, and understanding the mechanisms could potentially lead to new therapeutic approaches. The authors should make a stronger case for their protocol.</p>
<p>The discussion should include a section comparing generalization curves in mice trained with other conditioning procedures such as fear conditioning. The steepness of the generalization function is particularly important and might differ between eyeblink conditioning and other paradigms that use longer and/or louder tones.</p>
<p>Minor issues</p>
<p>The authors include 7 males and 7 females, but do not provide any information about the effect of sex in their study. Given the paucity of sex-balanced studies in neuroscience, it would be useful to document if there was an effect of sex or not. N=7 is for most behavioral tests lies on the low side (or n=11). Was a prospective power analysis performed to ensure that the study was not underpowered?</p>
<p>In the first paragraph of the Introduction, the authors cite a paper by Smith et al. 2007 as an example of an eyeblink conditioning study, even though it is a fear conditioning study.</p>
<p>The authors claim that their eyeblink conditioning paradigm is “in a more natural setting,” which makes little sense since the mouse is head-fixed and walking on a foam treadmill. Moreover, the use of locomotion is a confounding factor introducing variability unless one triggers trials when the mouse is in a particular state (e.g., during locomotion). Thus, the authors should simply describe the use of their paradigm as a precise way to measure conditioning, and remind the reader that it has been used in their previous studies.</p>
<p>In the methods section, Charisma is mentioned, but not what it is. Is it a type of glue?</p>
<p>For the generalization results, F statistics are presented but it is not clear if these are from ANOVAs on the LME.</p>
<p>In the first paragraph of the discussion the authors claim that “lower and higher frequencies resulted in CR peaks with slightly decreased and increased latencies” but there was only a significant effect for the highest frequency.</p>
<p>In the discussion of CR timing, it is important to note that mice differ from humans, rabbits, and rats in not showing a decrease in CR onset latency across training. This finding also differs from many other Pavlovian CRs, including salivation in Pavlov’s original experiments. Is there something about the combination of species and ISI that causes this difference?</p>
<p>Halverson et al. 2015 and 2018 should be cited as examples of studies showing simple spike pauses.</p>
<p>In several figures, the term “criterium” should be replaced with ’criteria’ or ’criterion’.</p>
<p>Figure S1 and Tables 1-2 may not all be needed. One possibility could be to keep Figure S1 and remove the tables.</p>
<p>Figure S4 is not necessary, as this correlation is well documented.</p></section><section id="ref-list1" class="ref-list"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">References</h2>
